GLUT1 Deficiency Syndrome

Mendelian MONDO:0000188 Pathograph 42 Show in embeddings browser Inborn Error of Metabolism Inborn Carbohydrate Metabolic Disorder Metabolic Epilepsy Epilepsy Movement Disorder

Glucose transporter type 1 (GLUT1) deficiency syndrome (Glut1DS) is a disorder of brain energy metabolism caused by heterozygous (rarely biallelic) pathogenic variants in SLC2A1, which encodes the GLUT1 facilitative glucose transporter responsible for transporting glucose across the endothelial cells of the blood-brain barrier and into erythrocytes. Reduced GLUT1 function starves the brain of its principal metabolic fuel, producing a cerebral energy deficit that manifests as an infantile-onset, often pharmacoresistant epileptic encephalopathy with acquired microcephaly, developmental delay, and a complex movement disorder (ataxia, dystonia, spasticity) in the classic severe form. Milder and later-onset presentations exist across a phenotypic spectrum, including paroxysmal exertion-induced dyskinesia (GLUT1 deficiency syndrome 2 / DYT18) and episodic choreoathetosis with spasticity (dystonia 9 / DYT9). The diagnostic hallmark is hypoglycorrhachia (low CSF glucose) with a low CSF:blood glucose ratio in the setting of normoglycemia. The ketogenic diet, which supplies ketone bodies as an alternative cerebral fuel bypassing the GLUT1 transport defect, is first-line disease-specific therapy.

Inheritance

2
Autosomal Dominant HP:0000006
Glut1DS is most commonly caused by a heterozygous SLC2A1 pathogenic variant inherited in an autosomal dominant manner. About 90% of individuals have the disorder as the result of a de novo SLC2A1 pathogenic variant; about 10% have a variant inherited from a parent, whose degree of impairment may be mild or nonexistent (possible parental somatic mosaicism). Rare biallelic (autosomal recessive) inheritance has also been reported.
Autosomal dominant inheritance Penetrance: INCOMPLETE Expressivity: VARIABLE De novo rate: About 90% de novo; about 10% inherited from a parent (GeneReviews)
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:20301603 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Glut1DS is most commonly caused by a heterozygous SLC2A1 pathogenic variant and inherited in an autosomal dominant manner. About 90% of individuals with Glut1DS have the disorder as the result of a de novo SLC2A1 pathogenic variant; about 10% of individuals have the disorder as the result of a..."
GeneReviews genetic counseling section directly documents autosomal dominant inheritance, predominantly de novo, with a minority of inherited cases.
PMID:11136715 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"This is the first report of autosomal dominant transmission of GLUT1 deficiency, confirming that this condition is the result of haploinsufficiency."
Father-to-child transmission in two separate marriages established dominant inheritance and the haploinsufficiency mechanism.
PMID:20301603 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The degree of impairment in the transmitting parent may be mild or nonexistent; parental somatic mosaicism for the SLC2A1 pathogenic variant may explain this observation."
Supports incomplete penetrance / variable expressivity in transmitting parents.
Autosomal Recessive HP:0000007
Rarely, biallelic SLC2A1 variants (compound heterozygous or homozygous) inherited from asymptomatic heterozygous parents produce Glut1DS as an autosomal recessive trait. The clinical inheritance pattern is determined by the relative pathogenicity of each allele and the resulting degree of GLUT1 haploinsufficiency, measured as residual erythrocyte glucose uptake.
Autosomal recessive inheritance
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:20687207 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"These cases demonstrate that Glut1 DS may present as an autosomal recessive trait. The clinical pattern of inheritance is determined by the relative pathogenicity of the mutation and the resulting degree of haploinsufficiency."
Two families with biallelic SLC2A1 variants and asymptomatic heterozygous parents define the recessive presentation.
PMID:20301603 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Autosomal recessive inheritance has been reported in two families to date."
GeneReviews confirms recessive inheritance is rare (two families).

Subtypes

2
Classic (Severe, Infantile-Onset) GLUT1 Deficiency Syndrome MONDO:0011724
The most common and severe presentation, with onset in infancy to early childhood. Predominant clinical findings are paroxysmal eye-head movements, pharmacoresistant seizures of varying types, deceleration of head growth, and developmental delay, followed by complex movement disorders and intellectual disability ranging from mild to severe.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:20301603 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"When first diagnosed in infancy to early childhood, the predominant clinical findings of Glut1DS are paroxysmal eye-head movements, pharmacoresistant seizures of varying types, deceleration of head growth, and developmental delay."
GeneReviews describes the classic infantile-onset presentation as the most common and severe form of Glut1DS.
GLUT1 Deficiency Syndrome 2 (DYT18 / Paroxysmal Exercise-Induced Dyskinesia, including DYT9 / episodic choreoathetosis with spasticity) MONDO:0012805
A milder, later-onset (childhood) form dominated by paroxysmal dystonic attacks of the extremities triggered by prolonged physical exertion, with or without epilepsy or hemolytic anemia. Corresponds to OMIM PS612126 / MONDO childhood-onset GLUT1 deficiency syndrome 2 (DYT18). This subtype also covers the presentation formerly designated dystonia 9 (DYT9, OMIM 601042, MONDO:0010983, episodic choreoathetosis with spasticity), in which the same exertion-induced dyskinesia is accompanied by slowly progressive spastic paraparesis: the German/Dutch DYT9 pedigree was shown to carry a causative SLC2A1 mutation, making DYT9 and DYT18 allelic rather than distinct entities.
Show evidence (4 references)
PMID:21649651 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"This paper also shows phenotypical parallels between GLUT1DS and paroxysmal exertion-induced dyskinesia."
Directly documents the milder paroxysmal exertion-induced dyskinesia phenotype as part of the GLUT1DS spectrum.
PMID:18577546 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"co-occurring PED and epilepsy can be due to autosomal dominant heterozygous SLC2A1 mutations, expanding the phenotypic spectrum associated with GLUT1 deficiency"
Five-generation family plus three nuclear families establish PED with epilepsy as an SLC2A1 phenotype.
PMID:18451999 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Their PED was accompanied by epilepsy, mild developmental delay, reduced CSF glucose levels, hemolytic anemia with echinocytosis, and altered erythrocyte ion concentrations."
Three-generation PED family documents the hemolytic-anemia variant of the GLUT1DS2 presentation.

Mechanistic Hypotheses

3
Blood-brain-barrier glucose transport failure causes cerebral energy deficit
canonical_glut1_energy_deficit CANONICAL
Evidence balance 2 support
Haploinsufficient GLUT1 at the brain endothelium limits glucose delivery, producing hypoglycorrhachia and a chronic brain energy deficit that is greatest when developmental glucose demand peaks; the deficit drives cortical hyperexcitability, impaired neurodevelopment and, under exertion or fasting, episodic basal ganglia failure. Ketogenic diet therapy works by supplying ketone bodies that bypass the transporter.
Pathograph links
SLC2A1 Loss-of-Function Variant -> Reduced GLUT1 Transporter Function at the Blood-Brain Barrier DIRECT Loss-of-function SLC2A1 variants reduce the density or activity of GLUT1 transporters expressed on brain microvascular endothelial cells.
Reduced GLUT1 Transporter Function at the Blood-Brain Barrier -> Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit DIRECT Insufficient transendothelial glucose flux starves brain tissue of its principal metabolic substrate, independent of normal peripheral blood glucose levels.
Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit -> Exertion- and Fasting-Sensitive Basal Ganglia Energy Failure DIRECT When energy demand rises (sustained exertion) or substrate supply falls (fasting), the marginal glucose supply to metabolically expensive basal ganglia circuits is transiently exceeded.
Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit -> Neuronal Hyperexcitability and Hypersynchrony INDIRECT KNOWN INTERMEDIATES Energy-deficient neurons and cortical circuits become hyperexcitable and hypersynchronous, generating the pharmacoresistant seizures characteristic of Glut1DS.
Exertion- and Fasting-Sensitive Basal Ganglia Energy Failure -> Paroxysmal Exercise/Exertion-Induced Dyskinesia DIRECT
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:32913944 SUPPORT Other
"Glut1 deficiency syndrome (Glut1DS) is a brain energy failure syndrome caused by impaired glucose transport across brain tissue barriers."
Consensus definition of the canonical mechanism.
PMID:9462754 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"These symptoms suggested the existence of a defect in glucose transport across the blood brain barrier."
The founding formulation of the hypothesis, confirmed by the mutations reported in the same paper.
GLUT1 deficiency arrests cerebral angiogenesis, adding a structural microvascular deficit
microvascular_developmental_defect EMERGING
Evidence balance 2 support
Beyond its fuel-transport role, endothelial GLUT1 is required for postnatal cerebral angiogenesis; in model mice low GLUT1 arrests microvessel growth and early, but not late, repletion averts disease. The consensus statement has adopted impaired microvascular development as a complication of delayed treatment, but the human contribution is unmeasured, and this model may partly explain why fuel substitution rescues seizures more completely than cognition.
Pathograph links
Reduced GLUT1 Transporter Function at the Blood-Brain Barrier -> Arrested Cerebral Angiogenesis and Microvascular Diminution DIRECT Beyond fuel delivery, endothelial GLUT1 is required for postnatal cerebral angiogenesis; low GLUT1 arrests microvessel expansion in model mice.
Arrested Cerebral Angiogenesis and Microvascular Diminution -> Impaired Neurodevelopment and Movement Disorder INDIRECT UNKNOWN INTERMEDIATES A diminished microvascular bed is proposed to compound the fuel deficit and contribute to cognitive dysfunction; the human contribution is unquantified.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:28106060 SUPPORT Model Organism
"We used model mice to demonstrate that low Glut1 protein arrests cerebral angiogenesis, resulting in a profound diminution of the brain microvasculature without compromising the blood-brain barrier."
Primary mouse evidence for the hypothesis.
PMID:40405536 SUPPORT Other
"Research reaches beyond the concept of brain energy depletion by impaired GLUT1-mediated glucose transfer across the blood-brain barrier."
2025 review frames non-fuel mechanisms as an active, still-emerging area.
Pore-region GLUT1 alleles cause hemolysis through a cation leak
erythrocyte_cation_leak_hemolysis EMERGING GLUT1 DS with Paroxysmal Exertion-Induced Dyskinesia
Evidence balance 1 support
Specific pore-region SLC2A1 alleles render the mutant transporter permeable to sodium, potassium and calcium; the resulting erythrocyte electrolyte disturbance produces echinocytosis and hemolytic anemia. Alleles that only reduce transport do not cause hemolysis, so this is an allele-specific gain of an abnormal function layered on haploinsufficiency, established in one family plus heterologous expression.
Pathograph links
Erythrocyte GLUT1 Dysfunction -> Hemolytic anemia DIRECT Restricted to alleles that create a cation leak (e.g. Q282_S285del); alleles that only reduce transport (A275T, G314S) do not cause hemolysis.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:18451999 SUPPORT In Vitro
"the hemolysis with echinocytosis may result from alterations in intracellular electrolytes caused by a cation leak through mutant GLUT1"
The authors' mechanistic proposal from oocyte and erythrocyte studies.

Discussions and Knowledge Gaps

4
Which cellular intermediate translates chronic cerebral glucose under-supply into the specific pattern of neuronal hyperexcitability and hypersynchrony seen in Glut1DS — failure of the astrocyte-neuron lactate shuttle, selective energy vulnerability of fast-spiking GABAergic interneurons, disturbed adenosine/thalamocortical signaling, or a combination?
KNOWLEDGE GAP OPEN gap_glut1_energy_deficit_to_hyperexcitability_intermediate
Attached to
pathophysiology#Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit pathophysiology#Neuronal Hyperexcitability and Hypersynchrony
The causal edge from "Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit" to "Neuronal Hyperexcitability and Hypersynchrony" is curated as INDIRECT_KNOWN_INTERMEDIATES, but the identity of that intermediate is not actually established. Glucose reaches the brain almost exclusively through GLUT1, yet why a global fuel shortfall produces the particular Glut1DS electroclinical signature (including 2.5-4 Hz generalized spike-wave and absence-like seizures) rather than diffuse depression is unresolved. Candidate mechanisms — astrocytic lactate-supply failure, preferential ATP starvation of parvalbumin interneurons whose high firing rates make them metabolically expensive, and adenosine-mediated network effects — carry different therapeutic implications, so resolving the intermediate would materially sharpen the mechanism model.
Proposed experiments
Cell-type-resolved metabolic-to-excitability mapping in Glut1 haploinsufficiency
cell-type-resolved metabolic and excitability mapping experiment Relation: this experiment is of type this experiment type This experiment is of type cell-type-resolved metabolic and excitability mapping experiment.
exp_glut1_celltype_metabolic_intermediate
In GLUT-1+/- mice and patient-derived iPSC neuron-astrocyte co-cultures, simultaneously measure astrocytic lactate output, interneuron-specific ATP/metabolic stress, and network excitability, then test whether interneuron-targeted metabolic rescue (or lactate supplementation) normalizes hypersynchrony independently of restoring bulk neuronal glucose.
Perturbations
Interneuron-selective metabolic rescue
pathophysiology#Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit
Selectively supply alternative fuel (lactate/ketone) or restore GLUT1 in astrocytes vs interneurons vs principal neurons to identify the rate-limiting compartment.

Network hyperexcitability

pathophysiology#Neuronal Hyperexcitability and Hypersynchrony
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:20301603 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Glucose, the essential metabolic fuel for the brain, is transported into the brain exclusively by the protein glucose transporter type 1 (Glut1) across the endothelial cells forming the blood-brain barrier (BBB)."
Establishes the exclusive GLUT1 route for cerebral glucose, framing why the deficit is global; the open question is which cell type first converts that global shortfall into focal-onset hyperexcitability.
PMID:16497725 SUPPORT Model Organism
"GLUT-1+/- mice have epileptiform discharges on electroencephalography (EEG), impaired motor activity, incoordination, hypoglycorrhachia, microencephaly, decreased brain glucose uptake as measured by positron emission tomography (PET) scan and decreased brain Glut-1 expression by western blot (66%)."
Provides a tractable in vivo system in which the epileptiform phenotype is reproduced, i.e., a model where the missing cellular intermediate could be dissected with cell-type-specific readouts.
Why does ketone-based fuel substitution robustly control seizures in Glut1DS yet only partially rescue the movement disorder and cognitive impairment — is the residual deficit a missed developmental window, regional or functional under-supply of ketone-derived fuel, or a non-fuel role of glucose that ketones cannot replace?
KNOWLEDGE GAP OPEN gap_glut1_ketogenic_seizure_vs_neurodevelopment_dissociation
Attached to
pathophysiology#Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit pathophysiology#Impaired Neurodevelopment and Movement Disorder pathophysiology#Recurrent Pharmacoresistant Seizures
Ketogenic diet therapy is the mainstay treatment and supplies ketone bodies as an alternative cerebral fuel that bypasses the GLUT1 transport defect. In practice it controls seizures far more completely than it reverses the paroxysmal movement disorder, ataxia/dystonia, or cognitive outcome, and outcomes are consistently better with earlier treatment. Whether the residual, seizure-independent morbidity reflects irreversible injury incurred before diagnosis (a developmental-window effect), inadequate ketone delivery to specific circuits, or a role of glucose beyond bulk fuel (e.g., biosynthetic/pentose-phosphate demands) is unresolved — and it determines whether earlier or supplemental (e.g., anaplerotic) therapy could close the gap.
Proposed experiments
Treatment-timing dissociation of seizure vs neurodevelopmental endpoints
treatment-timing endpoint dissociation experiment Relation: this experiment is of type this experiment type This experiment is of type treatment-timing endpoint dissociation experiment.
exp_glut1_treatment_timing_endpoint_dissociation
In GLUT-1+/- mice, initiate ketogenic therapy or GLUT1 repletion across a graded set of ages and measure seizure/EEG control separately from motor coordination, brain growth, and cognitive-behavioral endpoints, testing whether the neurodevelopmental endpoints have an earlier closing window than the seizure endpoint.
Perturbations
Age-graded ketogenic/repletion therapy
pathophysiology#Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit
Start fuel substitution or GLUT1 repletion at several developmental ages.

"Targeted therapy: Age-specific KDTs primarily provide a supplemental fuel, namely, ketone bodies, for brain energy metabolism."

PMID:20301603 SUPPORT Human Clinical

Documents the developmental-window dependence of outcome, one of the competing explanations for the residual neurodevelopmental deficit.
PMID:28106060 SUPPORT Model Organism
"Restoring the protein to 2-week old mutants, in which certain disease characteristics are readily apparent, is less effective in shaping normal brain microvasculature."
Model-organism evidence that later restoration incompletely reverses structural brain defects, supporting a developmental-window contribution to residual, seizure-independent morbidity.
+ 2 more references
Does the GLUT-1+/- mouse faithfully model the full human Glut1DS phenotype — in particular the paroxysmal features (paroxysmal eye-head movements, exercise-induced dyskinesia) and the spectrum extending to early-onset absence epilepsy and movement-disorder-predominant presentations — or does it capture mainly the core seizure/hypoglycorrhachia/microcephaly triad?
HUMAN MODEL MISMATCH OPEN gap_glut1_mouse_model_paroxysmal_phenotype_fidelity
Attached to
pathophysiology#Neuronal Hyperexcitability and Hypersynchrony pathophysiology#Impaired Neurodevelopment and Movement Disorder
The GLUT-1+/- mouse reproduces epileptiform EEG, motor impairment, hypoglycorrhachia and microencephaly and is described as mimicking the classical human presentation, making it the workhorse model for Glut1DS. But the human disorder is defined as much by paroxysmal, activity- and fasting-triggered phenomena and by a wide phenotypic spectrum (paroxysmal exercise-induced dyskinesia, early-onset absence epilepsy, isolated movement disorder) that depend on human-specific behavioral repertoire and developmental glucose-demand timing. Whether these paroxysmal and spectrum-defining features are recapitulated — and thus whether the model can validate therapies aimed at them — is the open translational question, distinct from a claim that evidence is simply absent.
Proposed experiments

PMID:16497725 SUPPORT Model Organism
"The GLUT-1+/- murine phenotype mimics the classical human presentation of Glut-1 DS."
Documents fidelity to the classical core phenotype; the open question is whether the paroxysmal and spectrum-defining human features are also captured.
Does SLC2A1 variant class (truncating or deletion versus missense) predict clinical severity in Glut1DS, or is severity set only by residual transporter activity irrespective of variant class?
CONTROVERSY OPEN controversy_glut1_genotype_phenotype_correlation
Attached to
pathophysiology#SLC2A1 Loss-of-Function Variant genetic#SLC2A1 has_subtypes#
The largest laboratory series reported that mutation type was related to the severity of intellectual disability and to the presence of complex movement disorders, whereas the Columbia series of 16 new patients and the 2007 review of 84 published cases found no correlation between genotype, phenotype and biochemical measures, and the French national cohort found overlapping phenotypes that resist genotype-phenotype assignment. What does correlate across series is residual erythrocyte glucose uptake, which even determines whether a family presents as dominant or recessive. The resolution matters for counselling and for how the has_subtypes entries are read: they are phenotype clusters on a continuum, not variant-defined entities.
Show evidence (5 references)
PMID:20129935 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Type of mutation was related to the severity of mental retardation and the presence of complex movement disorders."
Supports the claim that variant class predicts severity, in 57 genetically confirmed patients.
PMID:15622525 REFUTE Human Clinical
"There were no obvious correlations between phenotype, genotype, or biochemical measures."
Refutes the claim in an independent series of 16 patients.
PMID:17718830 REFUTE Human Clinical
"Autosomal dominant transmission and several mutational hot spots have been identified, but phenotype-genotype correlations are not yet apparent."
Review of 84 published cases found no apparent correlation.

Pathophysiology

9
SLC2A1 Loss-of-Function Variant
Heterozygous (rarely biallelic) pathogenic variants in SLC2A1 reduce the amount or function of the GLUT1 facilitative glucose transporter protein, typically through haploinsufficiency.
Blood-brain-barrier glucose transport failure causes cerebral energy deficit

Erythrocyte GLUT1 Dysfunction The same haploinsufficient transporter is expressed on erythrocytes, so red-cell hexose uptake falls in parallel with brain transport and serves as the accessible functional surrogate of the lesion.

PMID:20301603 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The diagnosis of Glut1DS is established in a proband with suggestive clinical findings, hypoglycorrhachia documented by lumbar puncture, and a (usually) heterozygous pathogenic variant in SLC2A1 identified by molecular genetic testing."
GeneReviews establishes heterozygous SLC2A1 pathogenic variants as the molecular cause of Glut1DS.
PMID:12029447 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Several heterozygous mutations resulting in GLUT1 haploinsufficiency have been identified."
Review paper documents the haploinsufficiency mechanism underlying heterozygous SLC2A1 mutations.
PMID:9462754 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"We now report two distinct classes of mutations as the molecular basis for the functional defect of glucose transport: hemizygosity of GLUT1 and nonsense mutations resulting in truncation of the GLUT-1 protein."
The founding molecular study identifies whole-gene hemizygosity and truncating nonsense alleles as the first two classes of causal SLC2A1 lesion.
+ 3 more references
Reduced GLUT1 Transporter Function at the Blood-Brain Barrier
GLUT1 is the transporter responsible for facilitated glucose diffusion across the endothelial cells that form the blood-brain barrier, as well as glucose uptake into erythrocytes. Reduced GLUT1 activity impairs the rate at which glucose can be delivered from blood to brain, independent of blood glucose concentration.
Blood-brain-barrier glucose transport failure causes cerebral energy deficit GLUT1 deficiency arrests cerebral angiogenesis, adding a structural microvascular deficit

International consensus statement (expert consensus, hence OTHER) restates the barrier-transport lesion as the disease-defining step.
Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit
Chronic under-supply of glucose to neurons and glia produces a state of cerebral energy insufficiency during the period of childhood brain development when glucose demand is highest, driving neuronal hyperexcitability, impaired neurodevelopment, and deceleration of brain (and hence head) growth.
Blood-brain-barrier glucose transport failure causes cerebral energy deficit

Exertion- and Fasting-Sensitive Basal Ganglia Energy Failure When energy demand rises (sustained exertion) or substrate supply falls (fasting), the marginal glucose supply to metabolically expensive basal ganglia circuits is transiently exceeded. Blood-brain-barrier glucose transport failure causes cerebral energy deficit
Reduced CSF lactate Lower glycolytic substrate delivery is the presumed reason CSF lactate is low-to-normal rather than raised; the intermediate steps are inferred, and the finding distinguishes Glut1DS from mitochondrial disease.
Paroxysmal eye-head movements Paroxysmal eye-head movements are among the earliest signs of the infantile energy deficit; the circuit through which they arise is not established.
Neuronal Hyperexcitability and Hypersynchrony Energy-deficient neurons and cortical circuits become hyperexcitable and hypersynchronous, generating the pharmacoresistant seizures characteristic of Glut1DS. Blood-brain-barrier glucose transport failure causes cerebral energy deficit
Impaired Neurodevelopment and Movement Disorder Sustained cerebral energy insufficiency during critical developmental windows impairs neurodevelopment and produces a complex movement disorder combining ataxia, dystonia, and spasticity, together with deceleration of head growth (acquired/progressive microcephaly).
Show evidence (4 references)
PMID:20301603 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The needs of the brain for glucose increase rapidly after birth, peaking in early childhood, remaining high until about age 10 years, then gradually decreasing throughout adolescence and plateauing in early adulthood."
GeneReviews explains why the cerebral energy deficit is most severe in infancy/early childhood when brain glucose demand peaks, framing the developmental basis of the classic phenotype.
PMID:26536893 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Furthermore, brain energy metabolism normalised with triheptanoin, that is, increased Pi/PCr ratio during brain activation compared to the recovery phase (p = 0.021), and deteriorated when triheptanoin was withdrawn."
31P-NMR spectroscopy in patients shows an abnormal activation-dependent brain energy profile that normalises with substrate supplementation, a direct in vivo measure of the cerebral energy deficit.
PMID:31399478 SUPPORT Model Organism
"glucose transportation was reduced, whereas hexokinase activity and glucose metabolism were enhanced"
FDG-PET kinetics in a missense mouse model separate the transport-limited supply step from a compensatory rise in downstream hexokinase flux.

Neuronal Hyperexcitability and Hypersynchrony
Cortical and subcortical neuronal networks deprived of adequate glucose fuel become hyperexcitable, producing hypersynchronous discharges that manifest as pharmacoresistant seizures of varying semiology. This node conforms to the shared epilepsy excitation-inhibition-imbalance final common pathway, substituting a metabolic (energy-deficit) upstream driver rather than a primary ion-channel lesion.

Impaired Neurodevelopment and Movement Disorder
Chronic cerebral energy deficit produces a complex, often paroxysmal movement disorder (ataxia, dystonia, spasticity), speech difficulty, and intellectual disability that can present alone or alongside epilepsy, reflecting the breadth of the GLUT1DS phenotypic spectrum.

Erythrocyte GLUT1 Dysfunction
Erythrocytes express the same GLUT1 transporter as the brain endothelium, so the haploinsufficient state is measurable outside the brain as reduced 3-O-methyl-D-glucose uptake (typically about half of control Vmax with an unchanged Km) and, with flow cytometry, as reduced surface GLUT1. Residual uptake tracks clinical severity. Certain pore-region alleles additionally render the mutant transporter leaky to cations, disturbing red-cell sodium, potassium and calcium and producing echinocytosis and hemolysis. This node is chiefly a diagnostic surrogate of the primary lesion; only the cation leak branch is itself pathogenic.
Pore-region GLUT1 alleles cause hemolysis through a cation leak

PMID:15622525 SUPPORT In Vitro
"The mean V(max) for the 3-O-methyl-D-glucose uptake into erythrocytes was 996 fmol/10(6) red blood cells per second, significantly less (54 +/- 11%; t test, p < 0.05) than the mean control value of 1,847. The mean Km value for the patient group (1.4 +/- 0.5mM) was similar to the control group..."
Kinetic assay in 16 patients shows roughly half-normal transport capacity with unchanged affinity, the erythrocyte signature of haploinsufficiency.
PMID:22190371 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"We also noted a significant correlation between the mean erythrocyte 3-OMG uptake and clinical severity (R(2) = 0.94)."
In 109 suspected cases, residual erythrocyte uptake correlates tightly with clinical severity.
PMID:18451999 SUPPORT In Vitro
"the hemolysis with echinocytosis may result from alterations in intracellular electrolytes caused by a cation leak through mutant GLUT1."
Identifies the cation-leak branch by which a pore-region GLUT1 allele damages the erythrocyte itself.
Arrested Cerebral Angiogenesis and Microvascular Diminution
In Glut1-deficient mice, low endothelial GLUT1 arrests postnatal cerebral angiogenesis and leaves a profoundly reduced brain microvasculature while barrier integrity is preserved. Pre-symptomatic AAV9-mediated GLUT1 repletion averts the vascular defect and the disease; late (adult) repletion does not. The 2020 consensus statement adopts impaired microvascular development as a complication of delayed treatment, but direct human vascular data are lacking, so this node is provisional and is the structural basis of an emerging, non-fuel mechanism.
GLUT1 deficiency arrests cerebral angiogenesis, adding a structural microvascular deficit

The consensus statement carries the mouse finding into the clinical framing of untreated early disease; it is expert opinion, not human vascular measurement.
Exertion- and Fasting-Sensitive Basal Ganglia Energy Failure
Under sustained exertion or fasting, the marginal glucose supply of the energy-deficient brain is transiently overwhelmed in corticostriatal circuits, producing episodes of choreoathetosis or dystonia affecting mainly the legs (paroxysmal exercise-induced dyskinesia). The same triggers aggravate seizures and paroxysmal ataxia. Functional imaging in PED families implicates altered glucose metabolism in the corticostriate pathways; the exertion-induced energy-deficit model is proposed rather than directly measured in the basal ganglia.
Blood-brain-barrier glucose transport failure causes cerebral energy deficit

Walking performance is a quantitative correlate of neurological burden in long-term follow-up.

Medical Actions

9
Ketogenic Diet Therapy
Category: Therapeutic Action: ketogenic diet intakeNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is ketogenic diet intake, annotated with Ketogenic Diet (NCIT:C173168). NCIT:C173168 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Ketogenic Diet NCIT:C173168
Platform: Behavioral / lifestyle
Age-specific ketogenic diet therapies (KDTs) supply ketone bodies as an alternative fuel for brain energy metabolism, bypassing the GLUT1 transport defect. Earlier initiation, ideally in infancy, is associated with better seizure control and long-term neurologic outcome. Effective for both seizure control and the movement disorder.
Mechanism Target:
BYPASSES Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:20301603 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Targeted therapy: Age-specific KDTs primarily provide a supplemental fuel, namely, ketone bodies, for brain energy metabolism."
GeneReviews describes the ketogenic diet mechanism of action as supplying an alternative cerebral fuel that bypasses the GLUT1 transport defect.
Target Phenotypes: Seizure HP:0001250 Human Phenotype Ontology (HP) Relation: this treatment targets this phenotype This treatment targets Seizure (HP:0001250). HP:0001250 is a phenotype from the Human Phenotype Ontology. Paroxysmal dyskinesia HP:0007166 Human Phenotype Ontology (HP) Relation: this treatment targets this phenotype This treatment targets Paroxysmal dyskinesia (HP:0007166). HP:0007166 is a phenotype from the Human Phenotype Ontology.
Show evidence (7 references)
PMID:36303089 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"All patients were seizure-free within a month of receiving the diet therapy. All patients were followed up for six months, three were followed up for 12 months after the treatment, and there was no recurrence of epilepsy during this period."
Retrospective single-center cohort of SLC2A1-mutation patients demonstrates rapid and durable seizure control with ketogenic diet therapy.
PMID:20301603 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Typically, the earlier the treatment the better the long-term clinical outcome."
GeneReviews documents that earlier ketogenic diet initiation yields better long-term neurologic outcome.
PMID:20129935 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"a ketogenic diet was effective in most of the patients with epilepsy (86%) and also reduced movement disorders in 48% of the patients with a classical phenotype and 71% of the patients with a non-classical phenotype."
Quantifies response of both seizures and movement disorder in 57 confirmed patients.

Modified Atkins Diet and other ketogenic dietary variants
Category: Therapeutic Action: modified Atkins and other ketogenic dietary therapiesNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is modified Atkins and other ketogenic dietary therapies, annotated with Dietary Intervention (NCIT:C15447). NCIT:C15447 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Dietary Intervention NCIT:C15447
Platform: Behavioral / lifestyle
Less restrictive ketogenic dietary therapies (modified Atkins diet, medium-chain-triglyceride diet, low-glycaemic-index treatment) are used, particularly in older children and adults, and in a large family survey gave seizure-freedom rates comparable to the classic 4:1 diet. They act by the same ketone-supply mechanism as the classic diet.
Mechanism Target:
BYPASSES Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit — Supplies ketone bodies as an alternative cerebral fuel, as the classic ketogenic diet does.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:26803281 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Diets used were extremely varied: 59 were treated with the classic ketogenic diet (KD), 29 with the Modified Atkins Diet (MAD), 4 with the Medium-chain Triglyceride (MCT) Diet and 2 with the low glycemic index treatment."
Documents real-world use of the dietary variants.
PMID:26803281 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"There was an equal percentage of children seizure-free receiving the KD/MCT Diets compared to the MAD/Low Glycemic Index Treatment (74% vs. 63%, p=0.30)."
No significant difference in seizure freedom between classic and less restrictive diets in this parent-reported survey.
Triheptanoin
Category: Therapeutic Action: PharmacotherapyNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is Pharmacotherapy (NCIT:C15986). NCIT:C15986 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. NCIT:C15986
Agent: Triheptanoin CHEBI:757060 Chemical Entities of Biological Interest (CHEBI) Relation: this treatment uses this therapeutic agent This treatment uses Triheptanoin (CHEBI:757060). CHEBI:757060 is a therapeutic agent from Chemical Entities of Biological Interest.
Platform: Small molecule
Triheptanoin is an anaplerotic odd-chain (C7) medium-chain triglyceride that provides Krebs-cycle substrates and an alternative brain fuel independent of GLUT1. An open-label study reported marked reduction of non-epileptic paroxysmal manifestations and normalized brain bioenergetics, but a subsequent randomized placebo-controlled trial did not show a significant reduction in seizure frequency in patients not on a ketogenic diet, leaving its efficacy for the epilepsy phenotype unproven.
Mechanism Target:
BYPASSES Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:26536893 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Treatment with triheptanoin resulted in a 90% clinical improvement in non-epileptic paroxysmal manifestations and a normalised brain bioenergetics profile in patients with GLUT1-DS."
Open-label study demonstrates triheptanoin supplies anaplerotic Krebs-cycle substrates that bypass the GLUT1 defect and normalize brain energy metabolism.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:26536893 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Treatment with triheptanoin resulted in a 90% clinical improvement in non-epileptic paroxysmal manifestations and a normalised brain bioenergetics profile in patients with GLUT1-DS."
Open-label pilot study supports triheptanoin efficacy against the non-epileptic paroxysmal (movement-disorder) manifestations of GLUT1DS.
PMID:35441706 REFUTE Human Clinical
"Triheptanoin did not significantly reduce seizure frequency in patients with Glut1DS not on the ketogenic diet."
Randomized double-blind placebo-controlled trial qualifies the treatment claim by showing no significant seizure-frequency benefit for the epilepsy phenotype.
Anti-Seizure Medications to Avoid
In individuals on ketogenic diet therapy, valproic acid should be avoided because it increases the risk of a Reye-like illness and may also inhibit glucose transport; phenobarbital, acetazolamide, topiramate, and zonisamide may be relatively contraindicated as adjunctive treatment. This is a "circumstance to avoid" (GeneReviews Agents/Circumstances to Avoid section) rather than a recommended treatment; retained here as a treatment-adjacent drug-safety note.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:20301603 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Agents/circumstances to avoid: In individuals on KDTs: (1) avoidance of treatment of seizures with valproic acid, because it increases the risk of a Reye-like illness and may also inhibit glucose transport; (2) other anti-seizure medications (ASMs) including phenobarbital, acetazolamide,..."
GeneReviews Agents/Circumstances to Avoid section directly documents these drug-safety warnings for GLUT1DS patients on ketogenic diet therapy.

Genetic Counseling
Category: Counseling / Informational Action: Genetic CounselingNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is Genetic Counseling (NCIT:C15240). NCIT:C15240 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. NCIT:C15240
Each child of an individual with autosomal dominant Glut1DS has a 50% chance of inheriting the SLC2A1 variant; because about 90% of cases are de novo and transmitting parents may be mildly affected or unaffected (possible mosaicism), parental testing is informative. Prenatal and preimplantation testing are possible once the familial variant is known.

AAV9-Mediated GLUT1 Gene Replacement (Experimental)
Category: Therapeutic Action: AAV9 SLC2A1 gene replacementNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is AAV9 SLC2A1 gene replacement, annotated with Gene Therapy (NCIT:C15238). NCIT:C15238 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Gene Therapy NCIT:C15238
Platform: Gene therapy
Experimental, preclinical only. In Glut1-deficient mice, pre-symptomatic AAV9-mediated repletion of GLUT1 averts the brain microvascular defect and prevents disease, and repletion in early-symptomatic animals still restores the microvasculature and ameliorates disease, whereas adult repletion gives no benefit. The 2020 consensus notes that interventions to restore GLUT1 protein content and function are in development. No human trial of GLUT1 gene replacement is cited here.
Mechanism Target:
RESTORES SLC2A1 Loss-of-Function Variant — Vector-delivered SLC2A1 restores GLUT1 protein content in the deficient brain.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:32913944 SUPPORT Other
"Ongoing research has identified future interventions to restore Glut1 protein content and function."
Consensus statement records protein-restoring interventions as the direction of therapeutic research.
RESTORES Arrested Cerebral Angiogenesis and Microvascular Diminution — Early GLUT1 repletion restores the cerebral microvasculature in model mice.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:28106060 SUPPORT Model Organism
"Glut1 repletion in early-symptomatic mutants that have experienced sustained periods of low brain glucose nevertheless restores the cerebral microvasculature and ameliorates disease."
Direct rescue of the vascular node by gene replacement in symptomatic mice.

Environmental Factors

2
Prolonged or strenuous exercise
exposure to prolonged or strenuous exercise ECTO:6000031 Environmental Conditions, Treatments and Exposures Ontology (ECTO) Relation: this environmental factor is this exposure This environmental factor is exposure to prolonged or strenuous exercise, annotated with exposure to strenuous exercise (ECTO:6000031). ECTO:6000031 is an exposure from the Environmental Conditions, Treatments and Exposures Ontology.
Sustained physical exertion is the defining trigger of paroxysmal exercise-induced dyskinesia and also aggravates paroxysmal ataxia, weakness and seizures. A standardised 6-Minute Walk Test reproducibly provokes episodes in long-term follow-up, and exertion sensitivity discriminates SLC2A1-positive from SLC2A1-negative patients.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:24789115 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The 6-Minute Walk Test captured gait disturbances and triggered paroxysmal exertional dyskinesia."
A standardised exertion protocol provokes the episodes.
PMID:25487684 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The factors that frequently aggravated these events were hunger, exercise, fever, and fatigue, in that order."
Exercise ranked second among aggravating factors in the nationwide cohort.
Mechanism Target:
TRIGGERS Exertion- and Fasting-Sensitive Basal Ganglia Energy Failure — Sustained muscular activity raises whole-body and cerebral glucose demand beyond what the haploinsufficient transporter can deliver to corticostriatal circuits.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:18451999 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"we propose that the dyskinesias result from an exertion-induced energy deficit that may cause episodic dysfunction of the basal ganglia"
The proposed mechanism by which exertion acts on this node.
Fasting and hunger
fasting Relation: this environmental factor is this exposure This environmental factor is fasting.
Deliberately left unbound. An ECTO search for fasting, food deprivation and starvation returned no exposure term, and the XCO candidates are experimental-condition terms rather than exposure events, so no term beats a wrong one. Bind when ECTO adds a fasting exposure.
Fasting or hunger lowers substrate supply to an already glucose-limited brain. Hunger was the most frequent aggravating factor for paroxysmal events in the Japanese nationwide cohort, fasting sensitivity discriminates SLC2A1-positive patients, and pre-prandial EEG abnormalities improve after feeding.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:25487684 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The factors that frequently aggravated these events were hunger, exercise, fever, and fatigue, in that order."
Hunger was the leading aggravating factor.
PMID:26193382 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Sensitivity to fasting or exertion in combination with those 3 main symptoms were the main differences between mutated and negative patients (p < 0.001)."
Fasting sensitivity is a discriminating clinical feature of SLC2A1-positive patients.
Mechanism Target:
EXACERBATES Exertion- and Fasting-Sensitive Basal Ganglia Energy Failure — Reduced blood glucose availability lowers an already marginal cerebral supply.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:23443458 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"A clue to the diagnosis in patients with paroxysmal symptoms may be the triggering of episodes during fasting or exercise."
Fasting as a trigger of the paroxysmal events produced by this node.

Biochemical Markers

4
Hypoglycorrhachia (Decreased)
Context: Low CSF glucose relative to blood glucose (CSF:blood glucose ratio typically below 0.4) in the setting of normoglycemia is the classic diagnostic biochemical signature of GLUT1DS, reflecting impaired GLUT1-mediated glucose transport across the blood-brain barrier.
Pathograph Readouts
Readout Of Reduced GLUT1 Transporter Function at the Blood-Brain Barrier Negative Diagnostic
A low CSF:blood glucose ratio reports reduced GLUT1-mediated glucose transport across the blood-brain barrier independent of systemic glucose levels.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:21649651 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"the presence of normoglycaemia with a CSF/blood glucose ratio of less than 0.4."
Directly defines the diagnostic CSF:blood glucose ratio cutoff used clinically.
Show evidence (5 references)
PMID:25487684 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The mean CSF/blood glucose ratio was 0.36 (0.28-0.48)."
Nationwide cohort of confirmed cases reports the empirical mean and range of the diagnostic CSF:blood glucose ratio.
PMID:12029447 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The hallmark of the disease is a low glucose concentration in the CSF (hypoglycorrhachia) in the presence of normoglycaemia (CSF/blood glucose ratio <0.4)."
Review confirms hypoglycorrhachia with CSF:blood glucose ratio below 0.4 as the diagnostic hallmark.
PMID:20129935 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Cerebrospinal fluid glucose was below 2.5 mmol/l (range 0.9-2.4 mmol/l) in all patients and cerebrospinal fluid : blood glucose ratio was below 0.50 in all but one patient (range 0.19-0.52)."
The ratio cut-off of 0.4 is not absolute; in 57 confirmed patients the ratio ranged up to 0.52, so a ratio below 0.5 with an absolute CSF glucose below 2.5 mmol/L is the practical screen.
+ 2 more references
CSF lactate (Low-to-normal)
Context: CSF lactate is low-to-normal (mean about 1.0 mmol/L versus a normal mean of 1.63 mmol/L; at or below the 10th percentile in 81% of reported patients) and is never elevated. Because raised CSF lactate is expected in mitochondrial encephalopathies, a low CSF lactate alongside low CSF glucose points to a transport rather than an oxidative defect.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:15622525 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The mean cerebrospinal fluid lactate concentration was 1.0 +/- 0.3mM, which was less than the normal mean value of 1.63mM."
Quantifies the lowered CSF lactate in 16 patients.
PMID:23999624 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"We conclude that if age-specific reference values are applied, CSF glucose and lactate levels are adequate biomarkers in the diagnostic workup of GLUT1 deficiency syndrome."
Systematic review endorses CSF lactate, with glucose, as a diagnostic biomarker when age-specific references are used.
Erythrocyte 3-O-methyl-D-glucose uptake (Decreased)
Context: Zero-trans uptake of the non-metabolisable glucose analogue 3-O-methyl-D-glucose into freshly isolated erythrocytes is reduced to about half of control Vmax with an unchanged Km. A cut-off of 74% of control uptake gives 99% sensitivity and 100% specificity against molecular confirmation, and residual uptake correlates with clinical severity, making it both a confirmatory functional test and a surrogate of the degree of haploinsufficiency.
Pathograph Readouts
Readout Of Erythrocyte GLUT1 Dysfunction Negative Diagnostic
Reduced uptake reports the transport capacity of the haploinsufficient GLUT1 population on the erythrocyte membrane.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:22190371 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"These findings validate the erythrocyte glucose uptake assay as a confirmatory functional test for Glut1 DS and as a surrogate marker for GLUT1 haploinsufficiency."
Establishes the assay as a surrogate of the transporter lesion.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:22190371 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The cut-off for an abnormally low uptake value was increased from 60% to 74% with a corresponding sensitivity of 99% and specificity of 100%."
Diagnostic performance of the uptake assay in 109 suspected cases.
PMID:15622525 SUPPORT In Vitro
"The mean V(max) for the 3-O-methyl-D-glucose uptake into erythrocytes was 996 fmol/10(6) red blood cells per second, significantly less (54 +/- 11%; t test, p < 0.05) than the mean control value of 1,847."
Kinetic quantification of the roughly 50% reduction in transport capacity.
Erythrocyte surface GLUT1 (METAglut1) (Decreased)
Context: Flow-cytometric quantification of GLUT1 on the erythrocyte surface (METAglut1) is a noninvasive blood test that is 80% sensitive and more than 99% specific for Glut1DS against lumbar puncture plus SLC2A1 analysis, and in one family with two inherited variants tracked the clinical phenotype better than functional uptake assays.
Pathograph Readouts
Readout Of Erythrocyte GLUT1 Dysfunction Negative Diagnostic
Reduced surface GLUT1 reports the reduced transporter content of the erythrocyte membrane.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:37076312 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"We wished to validate the diagnostic performance of METAglut1, a simple blood test that quantifies GLUT1 on the erythrocyte surface."
Defines what the test measures.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:37076312 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"METAglut1 was 80% sensitive and >99% specific for the diagnosis of Glut1DS."
Prospective multicentre validation (428 prospective and 67 retrospective patients).
PMID:31196579 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Instead, GLUT-1 expression at the surface of the erythrocytes appeared to better correlate with the clinical phenotypes in this family."
Single-family report in which surface expression, not uptake, tracked phenotype; a case-level observation, not a validation study.
🔬

Diagnosis

5
Lumbar Puncture with Paired CSF and Blood Glucose
Fasting lumbar puncture with simultaneous blood glucose measurement to demonstrate hypoglycorrhachia and calculate the CSF:blood glucose ratio, the key diagnostic biochemical test. CSF lactate should be measured on the same sample. Age-specific reference values, not a single ratio cut-off, should be applied.
lumbar puncture with paired CSF and blood glucose NCIT:C15327 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
Markers: CSF glucose, CSF:blood glucose ratio, CSF lactate
Results: CSF glucose typically below 2.5 mmol/L with a CSF:blood ratio below 0.5 and low-to-normal CSF lactate in the presence of normoglycemia.
Show evidence (4 references)
PMID:20129935 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Our study demonstrates that a lumbar puncture provides the diagnostic clue to glucose transporter-1 deficiency syndrome and can thereby dramatically reduce diagnostic delay to allow early start of the ketogenic diet."
Series of 57 patients with a mean diagnostic delay of 6.6 years shows lumbar puncture as the step that shortens it.
PMID:32913944 SUPPORT Other
"Diagnosis is confirmed by the presence of these clinical signs, hypoglycorrhachia documented by lumbar puncture, and genetic analysis showing pathogenic SLC2A1 variants."
Consensus diagnostic triad.
PMID:23999624 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"A typical CSF profile for GLUT1 deficiency syndrome, which is defined as a CSF glucose level at or below the 10th percentile, a CSF to blood glucose ratio at or below the 25th percentile, and a CSF lactate level at or below the 10th percentile, was found in only 35 of 4099 CSF samples (0.9%)..."
Specificity of the full CSF profile against a 4,099-sample database of other diagnoses.
+ 1 more reference
SLC2A1 Molecular Genetic Testing
Sequence analysis (and, if negative, gene-targeted deletion/duplication analysis such as MLPA) of SLC2A1 to identify the causative pathogenic variant. In the largest laboratory series 41% of referred requests were positive, and multi-exon deletions accounted for 6 of 49 distinct alleles, so deletion analysis is not optional.
SLC2A1 sequence and deletion/duplication analysis NCIT:C19770 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
Markers: SLC2A1 pathogenic variant (heterozygous; rarely biallelic)
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:20129935 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"From 2004-2008, 132 requests for mutational analysis of the SLC2A1 gene were studied by automated Sanger sequencing and multiplex ligation-dependent probe amplification. Mutations in the SLC2A1 gene were detected in 54 patients (41%) and subsequently in three clinically affected family members."
Defines the two-tier testing method and its yield in referred patients.
PMID:25487684 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Pathological mutations in the SLC2A1 gene were identified in 28 out of 32 cases (87.5%)."
Nationwide cohort demonstrates the diagnostic yield of SLC2A1 molecular testing in clinically suspected cases.
Erythrocyte GLUT1 Function Assay
The GLUT1 defect can be confirmed in erythrocytes by 3-O-methyl-D-glucose uptake studies and GLUT1 immunoreactivity, providing a functional confirmatory test alongside molecular genetic testing; at a 74%-of-control cut-off the uptake assay is 99% sensitive and 100% specific.
erythrocyte 3-O-methyl-D-glucose uptake assay NCIT:C18020 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
Markers: Erythrocyte 3-O-methyl-D-glucose uptake (% of control)
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:22190371 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The cut-off for an abnormally low uptake value was increased from 60% to 74% with a corresponding sensitivity of 99% and specificity of 100%."
Validation of the uptake assay's diagnostic performance in 109 suspected cases.
PMID:12029447 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The GLUT1 defect can be confirmed in erythrocytes by glucose uptake studies and GLUT1 immunoreactivity, and by molecular analysis of the GLUT1 gene."
Review documents erythrocyte glucose uptake and GLUT1 immunoreactivity as confirmatory functional diagnostic assays.
METAglut1 Erythrocyte Surface GLUT1 Blood Test
METAglut1 is a simple, noninvasive flow-cytometry blood test that quantifies GLUT1 protein on the surface of circulating erythrocytes, prospectively validated as a diagnostic test for GLUT1 deficiency syndrome and an alternative or complement to lumbar puncture. It can detect patients with SLC2A1 mosaicism and variants of unknown significance.
METAglut1 erythrocyte surface GLUT1 flow cytometry NCIT:C18020 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
Markers: Erythrocyte surface GLUT1 (flow cytometry)
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:37076312 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"METAglut1 was 80% sensitive and >99% specific for the diagnosis of Glut1DS."
Prospective multicenter validation reports the diagnostic sensitivity and specificity of the METAglut1 erythrocyte-surface GLUT1 blood test.
PMID:37076312 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"METAglut1 succeeded to identify patients with Glut1DS with SCL2A1 mosaicism and variants of unknown significance."
The blood test detects diagnostically challenging cases including SLC2A1 mosaicism and variants of unknown significance.
Electroencephalography
Routine and 24-hour EEG characterise seizure types and interictal discharges, but a normal interictal EEG is the most common finding at all ages, so EEG cannot exclude the diagnosis. A pre-prandial recording that improves after feeding is a supportive clue.
electroencephalography NCIT:C38054 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
Results: Normal interictal EEG most often; focal slowing or discharges in infants; generalized 2.5-4 Hz spike-wave from about 2 years, improving with food.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:12752470 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"In all ages, a normal interictal EEG was the most common EEG finding."
32 continuous 24-hour EEG sessions and 42 routine EEGs establish the low sensitivity of EEG.
🩻

Imaging Findings

1
Normal or nonspecific brain MRI
Structural neuroimaging is uninformative in Glut1DS. MRI is used to exclude structural lesions and other neurometabolic encephalopathies rather than to establish the diagnosis.
Mri
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:17718830 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Neuroimaging is uninformative. Most patients carry heterozygous de novo mutations in the GLUT1 gene"
Review of 84 published cases states that neuroimaging does not contribute to diagnosis.
📈

Progression

2
Infancy, epilepsy-dominant phase
Age: First months of life to early childhood
Seizures with a mean onset around 5 months, paroxysmal eye-head movements, developmental delay and deceleration of head growth dominate; 65% of classical patients present before 2 years. Seizures improve through childhood, particularly on ketogenic diet therapy.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:24789115 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Epilepsy dominated infancy and improved during childhood."
Longitudinal cohort defines the infantile epilepsy-dominant phase.
PMID:20129935 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"the classical phenotype (84%), subdivided into early-onset (<2 years) (65%) and late-onset (18%)"
Quantifies early versus late onset within the classical phenotype.
Childhood to adulthood, movement-disorder-dominant phase
Age: Later childhood, adolescence and adult life
Dystonia and paroxysmal exertion-induced dyskinesia emerge and become the dominant manifestations, with ataxia, spasticity, speech difficulty and intellectual disability persisting. Longitudinal neurological and neuropsychological measures remain stable rather than deteriorating, so the condition is chronic but not degenerative, and some individuals shift from a classic childhood phenotype to a movement-disorder-predominant adult one.
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:24789115 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"We conclude that Glut1 deficiency syndrome is a chronic condition, dominated by epilepsy in infancy and by movement disorders thereafter."
Defines the biphasic natural history.
PMID:24789115 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"All longitudinal outcomes remained stable over time."
Stability across a mean of 14.2 years of follow-up.
PMID:26193382 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Three patients evolved from a classic phenotype during early childhood to a movement disorder predominant phenotype at a late childhood/adulthood."
Documents the phenotype shift occurring within individuals.
📊

Prevalence

3
Danish population (SLC2A1 mutation carriers)
Point Prevalence 1.2 per 100,000 1–9 per 100,000
Estimated frequency of SLC2A1 mutations in the Danish population of approximately 1:83,000 (100000/83000 = 1.2 per 100,000), derived from mutation screening of absence-epilepsy and other cohorts; the authors call it an estimate of the frequency of GLUT1 deficiency syndrome and it is likely an underestimate given underdiagnosis.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:26537434 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"We estimated the frequency of SLC2A1 mutations in the Danish population to be approximately 1:83,000."
Population-level frequency estimate.
Scotland, children presenting with seizures before age 3 (live births)
Birth Prevalence 4.13 per 100,000 (1.07–7.19) 1–9 per 100,000 (births)
Minimum incidence of SLC2A1-related epilepsy of 1 per 24,300 live births (4.13 per 100,000; 95% CI 1.07-7.19) in a prospective national cohort of childhood-onset epilepsy; captures only the epilepsy-presenting fraction of Glut1DS diagnosed by age 3, so it is a floor for the birth prevalence of the syndrome as a whole.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:31302675 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"KCNQ2: 1 per 17 000 (5.89/100 000; 95% confidence interval 2.24-9.56) and SLC2A1: 1 per 24 300 (4.13/100 000; 95% confidence interval 1.07-7.19)."
Prospective population-based incidence of SLC2A1-related childhood epilepsy.
Japan (nationwide survey of board-certified pediatric neurologists)
Cases In Literature Rare
National case-ascertainment count (57 reported patients, 33 with detailed data), informative for rarity but not a normalised rate; likely an underestimate due to underdiagnosis.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:25487684 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"A questionnaire to survey the number of genetically and clinically confirmed cases of GLUT-1DS was sent to 1018 board-certified pediatric neurologists, which resulted in 57 patients being reported."
Nationwide Japanese survey ascertained 57 genetically/clinically confirmed cases nationally, informative for rarity but not directly convertible to a per-100,000 rate without further population denominators.
🔀

Differential Diagnoses

4

Conditions with similar clinical presentations that must be differentiated from GLUT1 Deficiency Syndrome:

Mitochondrial encephalopathies and other causes of low CSF glucose
Overlapping Features Low CSF glucose is more often seen acutely in CNS infection or malignancy than in a chronic neurological disorder, and in that chronic setting it is the diagnostic marker of Glut1DS. Mitochondrial encephalopathies can share seizures, movement disorder and developmental delay but characteristically raise CSF lactate, whereas in Glut1DS lactate is low-to-normal and blood glucose is normal.
Distinguishing Features
  • Low CSF glucose with normal blood glucose and low-to-normal CSF lactate favours Glut1DS.
  • Raised CSF lactate favours a mitochondrial encephalopathy.
  • Acute presentation with pleocytosis or malignant cells points to infectious or neoplastic causes of low CSF glucose.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:24323444 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"We present a practical overview on the differential diagnosis of a low CSF glucose and stress the importance of recognizing a low CSF glucose as the diagnostic marker for GLUT1 deficiency syndrome, a treatable neurometabolic disorder."
Frames low CSF glucose in chronic neurological disease as the Glut1DS marker to be separated from its other causes.
PMID:17718830 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"in the setting of normoglycaemia. CSF lactate is normal."
Normal (never raised) CSF lactate is the discriminating feature from mitochondrial disease.
Childhood absence epilepsy and other early-onset absence epilepsies
Overlapping Features Early-onset absence epilepsy without other features can be the sole presentation of an SLC2A1 variant; a Danish screen found SLC2A1 variants in 10% of absence-epilepsy patients, so Glut1DS should be excluded in absence epilepsy beginning before age four, especially with fasting or exertion sensitivity, developmental concerns or a movement disorder.
Distinguishing Features
  • Onset of absences before age 4, drug resistance, developmental concerns, movement disorder or fasting/exertion sensitivity favour SLC2A1 testing.
  • Hypoglycorrhachia on lumbar puncture separates Glut1DS from idiopathic absence epilepsy.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:26537434 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Mutations in SLC2A1 were detected in 5 (10%) of 50 patients with absence epilepsy, and in one (2.7%) of 37 patient with unselected epilepsies, ID, and/or various movement disorders."
Quantifies the overlap between absence epilepsy and Glut1DS.
Epilepsy with myoclonic-atonic seizures (Doose syndrome)
Overlapping Features Myoclonic-astatic epilepsy shares early-onset generalized seizures with Glut1DS, but a screen of 120 patients with myoclonic astatic epilepsy without associated movement disorder found no SLC2A1 variants.
Distinguishing Features
  • Absence of a movement disorder and normal CSF glucose argue against Glut1DS in myoclonic-atonic epilepsy.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:26537434 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"None of the 120 MAE patients harbored SLC2A1 mutations."
Negative screen separates typical MAE from Glut1DS.
Other paroxysmal dyskinesias (PRRT2-related kinesigenic and non-kinesigenic dyskinesias)
Overlapping Features Paroxysmal dyskinesias are classified by trigger; the exertion-induced (PED) form, provoked by prolonged exercise or fasting rather than sudden movement, is the one most likely to be Glut1DS, and a fasting or exercise trigger is the diagnostic clue.
Distinguishing Features
  • Episodes provoked by prolonged exertion or fasting, lasting minutes to an hour and affecting mainly the legs, favour SLC2A1-related PED.
  • Brief attacks provoked by sudden movement favour PRRT2-related kinesigenic dyskinesia.
  • A reduced CSF:blood glucose ratio (median about 0.5 in PED families) supports Glut1DS.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:23443458 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"A clue to the diagnosis in patients with paroxysmal symptoms may be the triggering of episodes during fasting or exercise."
Trigger profile as the discriminating clinical feature.
PMID:18451999 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"Paroxysmal dyskinesias are episodic movement disorders that can be inherited or are sporadic in nature."
Places SLC2A1-related PED within the broader paroxysmal dyskinesia group from which it must be distinguished.
🔬

Clinical Trials

1
NCT02014883 PHASE_II COMPLETED
Open-label phase II study of triheptanoin oil in eight patients with Glut1DS aged 7 to 47 years who declined or did not tolerate ketogenic diets, with baseline, treatment and withdrawal phases of two months each. It reported a 90% reduction in non-epileptic paroxysmal manifestations and a normalised brain bioenergetic profile on 31P-NMR. Completion is taken from the published results paper rather than from the registry summary.
Target Phenotypes: Paroxysmal dyskinesia HP:0007166 Human Phenotype Ontology (HP) Relation: this clinical trial targets this phenotype This clinical trial targets Paroxysmal dyskinesia (HP:0007166). HP:0007166 is a phenotype from the Human Phenotype Ontology.
Show evidence (2 references)
clinicaltrials:NCT02014883 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"The purpose of this project is to study the efficacy of triheptanoin oil in patients with GLUT1 deficiency syndrome."
Registry statement of the trial's objective.
PMID:26536893 SUPPORT Human Clinical
"We performed an open-label pilot study with three phases of 2 months each (baseline, treatment and withdrawal) in eight patients with GLUT1-DS (7-47 years old) with non-epileptic paroxysmal manifestations."
The results paper, which registers this NCT number, describes the trial design and population summarised here.

Animal Models

3
Glut1 haploinsufficient (GLUT-1+/-) mouse Genetically engineered
The first mouse model of Glut1DS: heterozygous disruption of the Glut1 promoter and exon 1 reduces brain Glut1 to 66% of wild type and produces epileptiform EEG discharges, impaired motor activity and incoordination, hypoglycorrhachia, microencephaly and reduced brain glucose uptake on PET, mimicking the classical human presentation.
Epileptiform EEG discharges Hypoglycorrhachia Microencephaly Impaired motor activity and incoordination Decreased brain glucose uptake on PET

Glut1 Rgsc200 ENU missense mouse Chemically induced by ENU mutagenesis
An ENU-induced missense allele at residue 324 of Glut1. Heterozygotes show decreased CSF glucose, contextual learning deficits, reduced body size, seizure-like behaviour, spontaneous EEG abnormalities and a longer wake and shorter NREM sleep pattern; FDG-PET kinetics show reduced glucose transport with enhanced hexokinase activity. Homozygotes die in utero, paralleling the presumed lethality of complete GLUT1 loss in humans.
Decreased cerebrospinal fluid glucose Seizure-like behaviour and abnormal EEG Contextual learning deficit Reduced body size Prolonged wake and shortened NREM sleep Embryonic lethality of homozygotes

Glut1-deficient mouse with AAV9-mediated Glut1 repletion Genetically engineered with gene-replacement rescue
Glut1-deficient model mice used to show that low Glut1 arrests cerebral angiogenesis and diminishes the brain microvasculature without breaching the blood-brain barrier, and that pre-symptomatic or early-symptomatic AAV9-mediated Glut1 repletion restores the microvasculature and prevents or ameliorates disease, whereas adult repletion does not.

Source YAML

click to show 
name: GLUT1 Deficiency Syndrome
creation_date: "2026-07-06T00:00:00Z"
category: Mendelian
description: >-
  Glucose transporter type 1 (GLUT1) deficiency syndrome (Glut1DS) is a disorder
  of brain energy metabolism caused by heterozygous (rarely biallelic) pathogenic
  variants in SLC2A1, which encodes the GLUT1 facilitative glucose transporter
  responsible for transporting glucose across the endothelial cells of the
  blood-brain barrier and into erythrocytes. Reduced GLUT1 function starves the
  brain of its principal metabolic fuel, producing a cerebral energy deficit that
  manifests as an infantile-onset, often pharmacoresistant epileptic
  encephalopathy with acquired microcephaly, developmental delay, and a complex
  movement disorder (ataxia, dystonia, spasticity) in the classic severe form.
  Milder and later-onset presentations exist across a phenotypic spectrum,
  including paroxysmal exertion-induced dyskinesia (GLUT1 deficiency syndrome 2 /
  DYT18) and episodic choreoathetosis with spasticity (dystonia 9 / DYT9). The
  diagnostic hallmark is hypoglycorrhachia (low CSF glucose) with a low CSF:blood
  glucose ratio in the setting of normoglycemia. The ketogenic diet, which
  supplies ketone bodies as an alternative cerebral fuel bypassing the GLUT1
  transport defect, is first-line disease-specific therapy.
synonyms:
- Glut1DS
- GLUT1DS
- GLUT1 DS
- De Vivo Disease
- Glucose Transporter Type 1 Deficiency Syndrome
- SLC2A1-related GLUT1 deficiency syndrome
- Encephalopathy due to GLUT1 Deficiency

- name: Autosomal Dominant

      id: HP:0000006

  description: >-
    Glut1DS is most commonly caused by a heterozygous SLC2A1 pathogenic variant
    inherited in an autosomal dominant manner. About 90% of individuals have the
    disorder as the result of a de novo SLC2A1 pathogenic variant; about 10%
    have a variant inherited from a parent, whose degree of impairment may be
    mild or nonexistent (possible parental somatic mosaicism). Rare biallelic
    (autosomal recessive) inheritance has also been reported.

    snippet: "The degree of impairment in the transmitting parent may be mild or nonexistent; parental somatic mosaicism for the SLC2A1 pathogenic variant may explain this observation."
    explanation: Supports incomplete penetrance / variable expressivity in transmitting parents.
  de_novo_rate: "About 90% de novo; about 10% inherited from a parent (GeneReviews)"

- name: Autosomal Recessive
  inheritance_term:
    preferred_term: Autosomal recessive inheritance
    term:
      id: HP:0000007
      label: Autosomal recessive inheritance
  description: >-
    Rarely, biallelic SLC2A1 variants (compound heterozygous or homozygous)
    inherited from asymptomatic heterozygous parents produce Glut1DS as an
    autosomal recessive trait. The clinical inheritance pattern is determined by
    the relative pathogenicity of each allele and the resulting degree of GLUT1
    haploinsufficiency, measured as residual erythrocyte glucose uptake.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:20687207
    reference_title: "Glut1 deficiency: inheritance pattern determined by haploinsufficiency."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "These cases demonstrate that Glut1 DS may present as an autosomal recessive trait. The clinical pattern of inheritance is determined by the relative pathogenicity of the mutation and the resulting degree of haploinsufficiency."
    explanation: Two families with biallelic SLC2A1 variants and asymptomatic heterozygous parents define the recessive presentation.
  - reference: PMID:20301603
    reference_title: "Glucose Transporter Type 1 Deficiency Syndrome."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Autosomal recessive inheritance has been reported in two families to date."
    explanation: GeneReviews confirms recessive inheritance is rare (two families).
pathophysiology:
- name: SLC2A1 Loss-of-Function Variant
  description: >-
    Heterozygous (rarely biallelic) pathogenic variants in SLC2A1 reduce the
    amount or function of the GLUT1 facilitative glucose transporter protein,
    typically through haploinsufficiency.
  gene:
    preferred_term: SLC2A1
    term:
      id: hgnc:11005
      label: SLC2A1
  role: trigger
  biological_scale: MOLECULAR
  mechanism_confidence: ESTABLISHED
  molecular_functions:
  - preferred_term: D-glucose transmembrane transporter activity
    term:
      id: GO:0055056
      label: D-glucose transmembrane transporter activity
    modifier: DECREASED
  notes: >-
    Variant classes span the whole loss-of-function spectrum. The founding
    molecular report identified hemizygosity (whole-gene deletion) and nonsense
    truncations; large series add missense, frameshift, splice-site,
    translation-initiation and multi-exon deletion alleles. Missense alleles can
    leave immunoreactive protein at the membrane while abolishing transport
    (Gly91Asp), and pore-region alleles (Q282_S285del) additionally create an
    erythrocyte cation leak. The degree of residual transport, not the variant
    class per se, sets clinical severity and even the apparent inheritance
    pattern.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:20301603
    reference_title: "Glucose Transporter Type 1 Deficiency Syndrome."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "The diagnosis of Glut1DS is established in a proband with suggestive clinical findings, hypoglycorrhachia documented by lumbar puncture, and a (usually) heterozygous pathogenic variant in SLC2A1 identified by molecular genetic testing."
    explanation: GeneReviews establishes heterozygous SLC2A1 pathogenic variants as the molecular cause of Glut1DS.
  - reference: PMID:12029447
    reference_title: "Facilitated glucose transporter protein type 1 (GLUT1) deficiency syndrome: impaired glucose transport into brain-- a review."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Several heterozygous mutations resulting in GLUT1 haploinsufficiency have been identified."
    explanation: Review paper documents the haploinsufficiency mechanism underlying heterozygous SLC2A1 mutations.
  - reference: PMID:9462754
    reference_title: "GLUT-1 deficiency syndrome caused by haploinsufficiency of the blood-brain barrier hexose carrier."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "We now report two distinct classes of mutations as the molecular basis for the functional defect of glucose transport: hemizygosity of GLUT1 and nonsense mutations resulting in truncation of the GLUT-1 protein."
    explanation: The founding molecular study identifies whole-gene hemizygosity and truncating nonsense alleles as the first two classes of causal SLC2A1 lesion.
  - reference: PMID:11136715
    reference_title: "Autosomal dominant transmission of GLUT1 deficiency."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: IN_VITRO
    snippet: "GLUT1 immunoreactivity was normal, but 3-O-methyl-D-glucose uptake into erythrocytes was significantly reduced, suggesting a quantitatively normal, but functionally impaired, GLUT1 protein at the cell membrane."
    explanation: A missense allele (Gly91Asp) shows that loss of transport function, not only loss of protein, produces the haploinsufficient state.
  - reference: PMID:18451999
    reference_title: "GLUT1 mutations are a cause of paroxysmal exertion-induced dyskinesias and induce hemolytic anemia by a cation leak."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: IN_VITRO
    snippet: "Functional studies in Xenopus oocytes and human erythrocytes revealed that this mutation decreased glucose transport and caused a cation leak that alters intracellular concentrations of sodium, potassium, and calcium."
    explanation: Heterologous expression confirms that PED-associated SLC2A1 alleles are loss-of-function for glucose transport, with an additional cation-leak gain in the pore-region deletion.
  - reference: PMID:20687207
    reference_title: "Glut1 deficiency: inheritance pattern determined by haploinsufficiency."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Red blood cell glucose uptake residual activity, a surrogate of haploinsufficiency, correlated with the clinical severity."
    explanation: The degree of residual transporter activity, rather than the variant class, scales the clinical phenotype.

- name: Reduced GLUT1 Transporter Function at the Blood-Brain Barrier
  description: >-
    GLUT1 is the transporter responsible for facilitated glucose diffusion
    across the endothelial cells that form the blood-brain barrier, as well as
    glucose uptake into erythrocytes. Reduced GLUT1 activity impairs the rate at
    which glucose can be delivered from blood to brain, independent of blood
    glucose concentration.

- name: Cerebral Glucose Energy Deficit
  description: >-
    Chronic under-supply of glucose to neurons and glia produces a state of
    cerebral energy insufficiency during the period of childhood brain
    development when glucose demand is highest, driving neuronal
    hyperexcitability, impaired neurodevelopment, and deceleration of brain (and
    hence head) growth.
  role: mediator
  biological_scale: TISSUE
  mechanism_confidence: ESTABLISHED

    explanation: GeneReviews explains why the cerebral energy deficit is most severe in infancy/early childhood when brain glucose demand peaks, framing the developmental basis of the classic phenotype.

    explanation: 31P-NMR spectroscopy in patients shows an abnormal activation-dependent brain energy profile that normalises with substrate supplementation, a direct in vivo measure of the cerebral energy deficit.

    explanation: Recent review frames energy depletion as the canonical model while flagging that astrocyte-neuron metabolic interactions are an active refinement of it.

- name: Neuronal Hyperexcitability and Hypersynchrony
  description: >-
    Cortical and subcortical neuronal networks deprived of adequate glucose fuel
    become hyperexcitable, producing hypersynchronous discharges that manifest
    as pharmacoresistant seizures of varying semiology. This node conforms to
    the shared epilepsy excitation-inhibition-imbalance final common pathway,
    substituting a metabolic (energy-deficit) upstream driver rather than a
    primary ion-channel lesion.
  role: central_effector
  biological_scale: TISSUE
  conforms_to: "epilepsy_excitation_inhibition_imbalance#Neuronal Hyperexcitability and Hypersynchrony"

    reference_title: "Seizure characterization and electroencephalographic features in Glut-1 deficiency syndrome."
    supports: SUPPORT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Seizure types were generalized tonic or clonic (14), absence (10), partial (nine), myoclonic (six), or astatic (four)."
    explanation: Chart review of 20 children enumerates the seizure semiologies that make up the endpoint.
  biological_scale: ORGANISM
  downstream:
